{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.15 0 C 2.15 1.19 1.19 2.15 0 2.15 C -1.19 2.15 -2.15 1.19 -2.15 0 C -2.15 -1.19 -1.19 -2.15 0 -2.15 C 1.19 -2.15 2.15 -1.19 2.15 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.33}{0.0}{0.0}{0.33}{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}
{{}{}}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.24 L 0 9.29}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{1.0}{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0pt}{6.9132pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\rangle
of all two-colored partitions, then
G
is the symmetric group
S_{n}
.

Currently, there is no complete list of all unitary easy quantum groups because the classification of all categories of two-colored partitions is not yet finished.

Remark 3.22
.

The names
\mathcal{O}
,
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{S}
of the four cases from Definition
3.8
were introduced by Tarrago and Weber in
[
34
, Definition 2.2]
and refer respectively to the orthogonal group
O_{n}
, bistochastic group
B_{n}
, hyperoctahedral group
H_{n}
and symmetric group
S_{n}
. (A “bistochastic” matrix is understood to be an orthogonal matrix each of whose rows and columns sums to
1
.) Tarrago and Weber showed that for each
\mathcal{X}\in\{\mathcal{O},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{S}\}
there exists a category of two-colored partitions which is case
\mathcal{X}
and maximally so. And, in the sense of Definition
3.18
, the maximal case-
\mathcal{O}
category is the one associated with
O_{n}
, the maximal case-
\mathcal{B}
category the one associated with
B_{n}
, the maximal case-
\mathcal{H}
category the one associated with
H_{n}
and the maximal case-
\mathcal{S}
category the one associated with
S_{n}
.

4
First Hochschild cohomology of universal algebras

For the convenience of the reader, Section
4
recalls the definition of and some elementary results about the first Hochschild cohomology. Throughout, let
\mathbb{K}
be any field.

4.1
First Hochschild cohomology

In Section
4.1
, our algebra shall remain abstract. Section
4.2
will then recall which conclusions can be drawn if a presentation of the algebra in terms of generators and relations is given.

Assumptions 4.1
.

Let
A
be any
\mathbb{K}
-algebra and
X
any
A
-bimodule.

That means in particular that
X
is a
\mathbb{K}
-vector space implicitly equipped with
\mathbb{K}
-linear maps
\triangleright\colon A\otimes X\to X
and
\triangleleft\colon X\otimes A\to X
, the left and right
actions
of
A
, such that
a_{1}\triangleright(a_{2}\triangleright x)=(a_{1}a_{2})\triangleright x
and
(x\triangleleft a_{2})\triangleleft a_{1}=x\triangleleft(a_{2}a_{1})
and
(a_{1}\triangleright x)\triangleleft a_{2}=a_{1}\triangleright(x\triangleleft a_{2})
for any
x\in X
and
\{a_{1},a_{2}\}\subseteq A
.

Example 4.2
.

For any augmentation
\epsilon
of
A
, i.e., any
\mathbb{K}
-algebra morphism from
A
to
\mathbb{K}
, the
\mathbb{K}
-vector space
\mathbb{K}
becomes an
A
-bimodule
X
if equipped with the
actions defined by
a\triangleright\lambda=\epsilon(a)\lambda
respectively
\lambda\triangleleft a=\lambda\epsilon(a)
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{K}
and
a\in A
. It is often called the
trivial bimodule
of
(A,\epsilon)
.

4.1.1
The fundamental definitions

The following definitions were first given by Hochschild in
[
16
]
.

Definition 4.3
.

(a)

The
X
-valued Hochschild
1
-cocycles of
A
are the
\mathbb{K}
-vector subspace
Z^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(A,X)
of
[A,X]
formed by all elements
\eta
such that for any
\{a_{1},a_{2}\}\subseteq A
,

\bigl(\partial^{1}\eta\bigr)(a_{1}\otimes a_{2}):=a_{1}\triangleright\eta(a_{2})-\eta(a_{1}a_{2})+\eta(a_{1})\triangleleft a_{2}=0.

(b)

The
X
-valued Hochschild
1
-coboundaries of
A
are the
\mathbb{K}
-vector subspace
B^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(A,X)
of
[A,X]
formed by all elements
\eta
such that there exists
x\in X
with for any
a\in A
,

\eta(a)=\bigl(\partial^{0}x\bigr)(a):=a\triangleright x-x\triangleleft a.

It can be seen that
\eta(1)=0
for any
\eta\in Z^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(A,X)
and that
B^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(A,X)
is a
\mathbb{K}
-vector subspace of
Z^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(A,X)
.

Definition 4.4
.

We call the quotient
\mathbb{K}
-vector space
H^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(A,X)
of
Z^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(A,X)
with respect to
B^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(A,X)
the
first Hochschild cohomology of
A
with
X
-coefficients
.

Example 4.5
.

In the case of Example
4.2
, i.e., for trivial coefficients, the only
1
-coboundary of
A
is the zero map because
(\partial^{0}\lambda)(a)=\epsilon(a)\lambda-\lambda\epsilon(a)=0
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{K}
and
a\in A
. Hence,
Z^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(A,X)\cong H^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(A,X)
in that instance.

4.1.2
Algebra hom characterization of 1-coycles

1
-cocycles can be characterized as certain algebra homomorphisms by means of a folk theorem recorded as
[
22
, Lemma 1.9]
. The latter uses the following construction.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
A\diamondplus^{1}_{0}X
denote the
\mathbb{K}
-vector space
A\oplus X
equipped with the
\mathbb{K}
-linear map
\mathbb{K}\to A\oplus X
with
1\mapsto(1,0)
and the
\mathbb{K}
-linear map
(A\oplus X)^{\otimes 2}\to A\oplus X
defined by for any
\{a_{1},a_{2}\}\subseteq A
and
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}\subseteq X
,

(a_{1},x_{1})\otimes(a_{2},x_{2})\mapsto(a_{1}a_{2},a_{1}\triangleright x_{2}+x_{1}\triangleleft a_{2}).

Lemma 4.7
.

A\diamondplus^{1}_{0}X
is a
\mathbb{K}
-algebra.

Proof.

(1,0)
is a unit because for any
a\in A
and any
x\in X
,

(a,x)\cdot(1,0)=(a1,a\triangleright 0+x\triangleleft 1)=(a,x)=(1a,1\triangleright x+0\triangleleft a)=(1,0)\cdot(a,x).

Moreover, for any
\{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\}\subseteq A
and any
\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\}\subseteq X
,

\displaystyle((a_{1},x_{1})\cdot(a_{2},x_{2}))\cdot(a_{3},x_{3})

\displaystyle{}=(a_{1}a_{2},a_{1}\triangleright x_{2}+x_{1}\triangleleft a_{2})\cdot(a_{3},x_{3})

\displaystyle{}=(a_{1}a_{2}a_{3},(a_{1}a_{2})\triangleright x_{3}+(a_{1}\triangleright x_{2}+x_{1}\triangleleft a_{2})\triangleleft a_{3})

\displaystyle{}=(a_{1}a_{2}a_{3},a_{1}a_{2}\triangleright x_{3}+a_{1}\triangleright x_{2}\triangleleft a_{3}+x_{1}\triangleleft a_{2}a_{3})

\displaystyle{}=(a_{1}a_{2}a_{3},a_{1}\triangleright(a_{2}\triangleright x_{3}+x_{2}\triangleleft a_{3})+x_{1}\triangleleft(a_{2}a_{3}))

\displaystyle{}=(a_{1},x_{1})\cdot(a_{2}a_{3},a_{2}\triangleright x_{3}+x_{2}\triangleleft a_{3})

\displaystyle{}=(a_{1},x_{1})\cdot((a_{2},x_{2})\cdot(a_{3},x_{3})),

which shows that the multiplication is associative.
∎

The following is then the folk theorem mentioned in
[
22
, Lemma 1.9]
.

Lemma 4.8
.

For any
\psi\in[A,X]
, the map
A\to A\oplus X
with
a\mapsto(a,\psi(a))
for any
a\in A
defines a
\mathbb{K}
-algebra homomorphism
A\to A\diamondplus^{1}_{0}X
if and only if
\psi\in Z^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(A,X)
.

Proof.

If the map in the claim is denoted by
f_{\psi}
, then
f_{\psi}(1)=(1,\psi(1))
and for any
\{a_{1},a_{2}\}\subseteq A
, obviously,
f_{\psi}(a_{1}a_{2})=(a_{1}a_{2},\psi(a_{1}a_{2}))
and

f_{\psi}(a_{1})\cdot f_{\psi}(a_{2})=(a_{1},\psi(a_{1}))\cdot(a_{2},\psi(a_{2}))=(a_{1}a_{2},a_{1}\triangleright\psi(a_{2})+\psi(a_{1})\triangleleft a_{2}).

The two values coincide if and only if
\psi(a_{1}a_{2})=a_{1}\triangleright\psi(a_{2})+\psi(a_{1})\triangleleft a_{2}
, which is to say if and only if
\psi\in Z^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(A,X)
. As then
\psi(1)=0
the claim is true.
∎

The next result will be required later in the proof of, ultimately, Proposition
4.19
.

Lemma 4.9
.

For any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, any
\{a_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\subseteq A
and any
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\subseteq X
, in
A\diamondplus^{1}_{0}X
,

{\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{i=1}^{m}(a_{i},x_{i})=\left({\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{i=1}^{m}a_{i},\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left({\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{j=1}^{i-1}a_{j}\right)\triangleright x_{i}\triangleleft\left({\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{j=i+1}^{m}a_{j}\right)\right).

Proof.

The cases
m\in\{1,2,3\}
are, respectively, trivial, the definition of the multiplication of
A\diamondplus^{1}_{0}X
and an intermediate result in the proof of Lemma
4.7
. Generally,

\displaystyle\left({\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{i=1}^{m-1}a_{i},{\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}}\left({\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{j=1}^{i-1}a_{j}\right)\triangleright x_{i}\triangleleft\left({\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{j=i+1}^{m-1}a_{j}\right)\right)\cdot(a_{m},x_{m})

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\left(\!\left({\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{i=1}^{m-1}a_{i}\right)a_{m},\left({\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{i=1}^{m-1}a_{i}\right)\triangleright x_{m}+{\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}}\left({\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{j=1}^{i-1}a_{j}\right)\triangleright x_{i}\triangleleft\left({\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{j=i+1}^{m-1}a_{j}\right)\triangleleft a_{m}\!\right).

Hence, the claim is true.
∎

4.2
Conclusions for universal algebras

Using Lemma
4.8
, it is possible to give a canonical equational characterization of the
1
-cocycles if a presentation of the algebra in terms of generators and relations is given.

Assumptions 4.10
.

Let
E
be any set,
R\subseteq\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle
arbitrary,
J
the two-sided ideal of
\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle
generated by
R
, and
X
any
\mathbb{K}\langle E\mid R\rangle
-bimodule.

Definition 4.11
.

Let

F^{1}_{E,R,X}\colon\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle\to[X^{\times E},X],\,p\mapsto F^{1,p}_{E,R,X}

be the unique
\mathbb{K}
-linear map with for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
and any
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\subseteq E
, if
p={\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{i=1}^{m}{e_{i}}
, then for any
{x\in X^{\times E}}
,

F^{1,p}_{E,R,X}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left({\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{j=1}^{i-1}{e_{j}}+J\right)\triangleright x_{e_{i}}\triangleleft\left({\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{j=i+1}^{m}{e_{j}}+J\right),

and with
F^{1,1}_{E,R,X}=0
.

Example 4.12
.

For any augmentation
\epsilon
of
A=\mathbb{K}\langle E\mid R\rangle
, if
X
is the trivial bimodule of
(A,\epsilon)
in the sense of Example
4.2
, then
for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
and any
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\subseteq E
, if
p=e_{1}\cdots e_{m}
, then
F^{1,p}_{E,R,X}
is a linear map which assigns to any family
x=(x_{e})_{e\in E}
of elements of
\mathbb{K}
the number

F^{1,p}_{E,R,X}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\Biggl(\prod_{j\in\llbracket{m}\rrbracket\backslash\{i\}}\epsilon\left(e_{j}+J\right)\Biggr)x_{e_{i}}.

Definition 4.13
.

(a)

Let
Z^{1}_{E,R,X}
denote the
\mathbb{K}
-vector subspace of
X^{\times E}
of all elements
x
with
F^{1,r}_{E,R,X}(x)=0
for any
r\in R
.

(b)

Write
B^{1}_{E,R,X}
for the
\mathbb{K}
-vector subspace of
X^{\times E}
formed by all elements
x
for which there exists
z\in X
with
x_{e}=(e+J)\triangleright z-z\triangleleft(e+J)
for any
e\in E
.

Lemma 4.14
.

For any
p\in\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle
and any
z\in X
, if
x\in X^{\times E}
is such that
x_{e}=(e+J)\triangleright z-z\triangleleft(e+J)
for any
e\in E
, then

F^{1,p}_{E,R,X}(x)=(p+J)\triangleright z-z\triangleleft(p+J).

In particular,
B^{1}_{E,R,X}
is a
\mathbb{K}
-vector subspace of
Z^{1}_{E,R,X}
.

Proof.

The claimed identity is clear if
p=1
. If there are
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\subseteq E
with
p=e_{1}\cdots e_{m}
, then by definition,

\displaystyle F^{1,p}_{E,R,X}(x)

\displaystyle{}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left({\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{j=1}^{i-1}{e_{j}+J}\right)\triangleright x_{e_{i}}\triangleleft\left({\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{j=i+1}^{m}{e_{j}+J}\right)

\displaystyle{}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left({\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{j=1}^{i-1}{e_{j}+J}\right)\triangleright((e_{i}+J)\triangleright z-z\triangleleft(e_{i}+J))\triangleleft\left({\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{j=i+1}^{m}{e_{j}+J}\right)

\displaystyle{}=\left(\sum_{i=2}^{m+1}\left({\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{j=1}^{i-1}{e_{j}+J}\right)\triangleright z\triangleleft\left({\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{j=i}^{m}{e_{j}+J}\right)\right)

\displaystyle{}\quad-\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left({\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{j=1}^{i-1}{e_{j}+J}\right)\triangleright z\triangleleft\left({\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{j=i}^{m}{e_{j}+J}\right)\right)

\displaystyle{}=\left({\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{i=1}^{m}{e_{i}+J}\right)\triangleright z-z\triangleleft\left({\overrightarrow{\prod}}\vphantom{\prod}_{i=1}^{m}{e_{i}+J}\right).

Thus, the identity holds for arbitrary
p\in\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle
by
\mathbb{K}
-linearity. It follows in particular that, if
p\in R
, then
F^{1,p}_{E,R,X}(x)=0
since
p+J
is the zero vector of
\mathbb{K}\langle E\mid R\rangle
in this case.
That proves the claim about
B^{1}_{E,R,X}
.

∎

In particular, the preceding lemma enables us to consider the following space.

Definition 4.15
.

Let
H^{1}_{E,R,X}
be the
\mathbb{K}
-vector quotient space of
Z^{1}_{E,R,X}
with respect to
B^{1}_{E,R,X}
.

The following notation allows referencing easily a multitude of algebra morphisms whose existence is implied by the universal property of
\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle
.

Notation 4.16
.

Let
B
be any
\mathbb{K}
-algebra and let
(b_{e})_{e\in E}\in B^{\times E}
be arbitrary. The
evaluation
of
p
at
(b_{e})_{e\in E}
in
B
is given by
p((b_{e})_{e\in E}):=g(p)
, where
g
is the unique
\mathbb{K}
-algebra morphism
\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle\to B
with
e\mapsto b_{e}
for any
e\in E
.

Lemma 4.17
.

For any
p\in\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle
and any
x\in X^{\times E}
, evaluating
p
at
(e+J,x_{e})_{e\in E}
in the algebra
\mathbb{K}\langle E\mid R\rangle\diamondplus^{1}_{0}X
yields

p((e+J,x_{e})_{e\in E})=(p+J,F^{1,p}_{E,R,X}(x)).

Proof.

Because
F^{1,1}_{E,R,X}(x)=0
by definition, the claim is true if
p=1
. If there exist
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\subseteq E
with
p=e_{1}\cdots e_{m}
, then the claim follows immediately from Lemma
4.9
and Definition
4.11
. For arbitrary
p
, the assertion therefore holds by
\mathbb{K}
-linearity.
∎

Remark 4.18
.

Given any
\mathbb{K}
-algebra
B
, any
\mathbb{K}
-algebra morphism
f\colon\mathbb{K}\langle E\mid R\rangle\to B
and any
p\in\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle
,

f(p+J)=p((f(e+J))_{e\in E}),

where the right-hand side is an evaluation of
p
in
B
.

Indeed, if
g
is the unique
\mathbb{K}
-algebra morphism
\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle\to B
with
e\mapsto b_{e}:=f(e+J)
for any
e\in E
, then
f(p+J)=g(p)=p((b_{e})_{e\in E})=p((f(e+J))_{e\in E})
, where the first identity holds by the uniqueness of
g
and where the second is nothing but an application of Notation
4.16
.

Now we can give a useful characterization of the spaces of
1
-cocycles of universal algebras.

Proposition 4.19
.

(a)

A commutative diagram of
\mathbb{K}
-linear maps is given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(\mathbb{K}\langle E\mid R\rangle,X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{1}_{E,R,X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(\mathbb{K}\langle E\mid R\rangle,X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{1}_{E,R,X},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}

where the horizontal arrows both assign to any element
\eta
of their respective domains the tuple

(\eta(e+J))_{e\in E}.

Moreover, the horizontal arrows are both
\mathbb{K}
-linear isomorphisms. Their respective inverses both assign to any element
x
of their respective domains the mapping
\mathbb{K}\langle E\mid R\rangle\to X
with

p+J\mapsto F^{1,p}_{E,R,X}(x)

for any
p\in\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle
.

(b)

There exists an isomorphism of
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle H^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(\mathbb{K}\langle E\mid R\rangle,X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{E,R,X}}

such that the class of any
\eta\in Z^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(\mathbb{K}\langle E\mid R\rangle,X)
is sent to the class of the
x\in Z^{1}_{E,R,X}
with
x_{e}=\eta(e+J)
for any
e\in E
.
The inverse isomorphism sends the class of any
x\in Z^{1}_{E,R,X}
to the class of the
\eta\in Z^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(\mathbb{K}\langle E\mid R\rangle,X)
with
\eta(p+J)=F^{1,p}_{E,R,X}(x)
for any
p\in\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle
.

Proof.

(a)
Abbreviate
A:=\mathbb{K}\langle E\mid R\rangle
and
B:=A\diamondplus^{1}_{0}X
.

Step 1. Upper horizontal arrow is well defined.
(
And a bit more
.)

First, we prove that for any
\eta\in Z^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(A,X)
, if
x_{e}:=\eta(e+J)
for any
e\in E
, then
\eta(p+J)=F^{1,p}_{E,R,X}(x)
for any
p\in\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle
. That then in particular shows that
F^{1,r}_{E,R,X}(x)=0
for any
r\in R
.

By
\eta\in Z^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(A,X)
,
according to Lemma
4.8
, the rule that
a\mapsto(a,\eta(a))
for any
a\in A
defines a
\mathbb{K}
-algebra homomorphism
f\colon A\to B
. Hence, for any
p\in\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle
it must hold that
(p+J,\eta(p+J))=f(p+J)=p((f(e+J))_{e\in E})=p((e+J,\eta(e+J))_{e\in E})=p((e+J,x_{e})_{e\in E})=(p+J,F^{1,p}_{E,R,X}(x))
in
B
, where the second and last identities are implied by Remark
4.18
and Lemma
4.17
, respectively. Hence,
F^{1,p}_{E,R,X}(x)=\eta(p+J)
for any
p\in\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle
, as claimed.

Step 2. Alleged inverse upper horizontal arrow well defined.

Next, we show that for any
x\in X^{\times E}
with
F^{1,r}_{E,R,X}(x)=0
for any
r\in R
there exists
\eta\in Z^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(A,X)
with
\eta(p+J)=F^{1,p}_{E,R,X}(x)
for any
p\in\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle
. One consequence of this is then that the alleged inverse upper horizontal arrow is well defined.

For any
r\in R
because
F^{1,r}_{E,R,X}(x)=0
and
r\in J
we can infer by Lemma
4.17
that
r((e+J,x_{e})_{e\in E})=(J,0)
in
B
. The universal property of
A
therefore guarantees the existence of a unique
\mathbb{K}
-algebra homomorphism
f\colon A\to B
with
f(e+J)=(e+J,x_{e})
for any
e\in E
. More generally, for any
p\in\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle
it must hold that
f(p+J)=p((f(e+J))_{e\in E})=(p+J,F^{1,p}_{E,R,X}(x))
, where the two identities are again due to Remark
4.18
and Lemma
4.17
. In other words, if
\eta(p+J):=F^{1,p}_{E,R,X}(x)
for any
p\in\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle
, then the rule that
a\mapsto(a,\eta(a))
for any
a\in A
defines a
\mathbb{K}
-algebra homomorphism
A\to B
, namely
f
. According to Lemma
4.8
, that demands
\eta\in Z^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(A,X)
. Hence, the initial claim is true.

Step 3. Upper horizontal arrow has alleged inverse.
It suffices to prove that for any
\eta\in Z^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(A,X)
and any
x\in X^{\times E}
with
F^{1,r}_{E,R,X}(x)=0
for any
r\in R
the statements that
x_{e}=\eta(e+J)
for any
e\in E
and that
\eta(p+J)=F^{1,p}_{E,R,X}(x)
for any
p\in\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle
are equivalent. Clearly, the second implies the first by the fact that
F^{1,e}_{E,R,X}(x)=x_{e}
for any
e\in E
by definition. And that the other implication holds was shown in Step 1.

Step 4. Vertical arrows well defined.
That the left vertical arrow is well defined is clear. That the same is true for the right vertical arrow was shown in Lemma
4.14
.

Step 5. Lower horizontal arrow and its inverse.
From the definition of the lower horizontal arrow and that of
B^{1}_{E,R,X}
, it is clear that the lower horizontal arrow is well defined. Conversely, the inverse of the upper horizontal arrow restricts to the inverse of the lower horizontal arrow. That is because for any
x\in X^{\times E}
with
F^{1,r}_{E,R,X}(x)=0
for any
r\in R
, for the unique
\eta\in Z^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(A,X)
with
\eta(p+J)=F^{1,p}_{E,R,X}(x)
for any
p\in\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle
and for any
z\in X
, if
x_{e}=(e+J)\triangleright z-z\triangleleft(e+J)
for any
e\in E
, then
\eta(p+J)=F^{1,p}_{E,R,X}(x)=(p+J)\triangleright z-z\triangleleft(p+J)=(\partial^{0}z)(p+J)
by Lemma
4.14
.

Step 6. Commutativity of the diagram.
Because the two horizontal arrows are defined by the same rule and since the vertical arrows are set inclusions the diagram commutes.

(b) Follows directly from

(
a
)

and is only stated for emphasis.
∎

Example 4.20
.

Let
X
be the trivial bimodule with respect to an augmentation
\epsilon
of
\mathbb{K}\langle E\mid R\rangle
as in Example
4.2
.
Because then
B^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(\mathbb{K}\langle E\mid R\rangle,X)=\{0\}
by Example
4.5
what Proposition
4.19
implies is that also
B^{1}_{E,R,X}=\{0\}
and that therefore
H^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(\mathbb{K}\langle E\mid R\rangle,X)\cong Z^{1}_{E,R,X}
.

Remark 4.21
.

If
V
is the
\mathbb{K}
-vector space underlying the
\mathbb{K}\langle E\mid R\rangle
-bimodule
X
, then the rules
p\blacktriangleright v:=(p+J)\triangleright v
and
v\blacktriangleleft p:=v\triangleleft(p+J)
for any
p\in\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle
and
v\in V
define left respectively right
\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle
-actions
\blacktriangleright
and
\blacktriangleleft
on
V
which turn it into a
\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle
-bimodule
Y
, the
restriction of scalars
of
X
along the canonical projection
\mathbb{K}\langle E\rangle\to\mathbb{K}\langle E\mid R\rangle
. With this definition there is no difference between the linear maps
F^{1}_{E,R,X}
and
F^{1}_{E,\varnothing,Y}
. (But, of course, there is in general still a difference between
Z^{1}_{E,\varnothing,Y}=V^{\times E}
and
Z^{1}_{E,R,X}=\{x\in V^{\times E}\,\wedge\,\forall r\in R\colon F^{1,r}_{E,\varnothing,Y}(x)=0\}
and likewise between
B^{1}_{E,\varnothing,Y}
and
B^{1}_{E,R,X}
.) The advantage of the notation
F^{1}_{E,R,X}
is that one can work immediately with the given bimodule
X
and does not have to introduce
Y
first. Then again, talking about
F^{1}_{E,\varnothing,Y}
can be advantageous too, e.g., in the instance of considering simultaneously multiple different
R
and thus multiple different
X
for which though the restrictions of scalars
Y
all happen to be the same.

5
Certain spaces of scalar matrices and their dimensions

The vector spaces of matrices appearing in the main result are characterized and their dimensions are computed.
Recall that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
any
v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
is called
skew-symmetric
if
v=-v^{\mathrm{t}}
.

Definition 5.1
.

We call any
v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})

small
if
\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{j,i}=0
for any
j\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
and
\sum_{j=1}^{n}v_{j,i}=0
for any
i\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
, i.e., if each row and each column sums to zero.

Lemma 5.2
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
the following equivalences hold.

(a)

There is
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
such that
v-\lambda I
is small if and only if
\sum_{s=1}^{n}v_{j,s}-\sum_{s=1}^{n}v_{s,i}=0
for any
\{i,j\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
. Moreover, then
\lambda=\sum_{s=1}^{n}v_{j,s}=\sum_{s=1}^{n}v_{s,i}
for any
\{i,j\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
.

(b)

There is
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
such that
v-\lambda I
is skew-symmetric if and only if for any
\{i,j\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
with
i\neq j
both
v_{j,i}+v_{i,j}=0
and
v_{j,j}-v_{i,i}=0
.
Moreover, then
\lambda=v_{i,i}
for any
i\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
.

(c)

There are
\{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\}\subseteq\mathbb{C}
such that
v-\lambda_{1}I
is skew-symmetric and
v-\lambda_{2}I
small if and only if there is
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
such that
v-\lambda I
is both skew-symmetric and small. Moreover, then
\lambda=\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}
.

Proof.

For
n=1
, all claims hold trivially. Hence, suppose
2\leq n
in the following.

(a)
If
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
is such that
w:=v-\lambda I
is small, then for any
\{i,j\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
it follows
0=\sum_{s=1}^{n}w_{j,s}=\sum_{s=1}^{n}(v_{j,s}-\lambda\delta_{j,s})=\sum_{s=1}^{n}v_{j,s}-\lambda
and
0=\sum_{s=1}^{n}w_{s,i}=\sum_{s=1}^{n}(v_{s,i}-\lambda\delta_{s,i})=\sum_{s=1}^{n}v_{s,i}-\lambda
, which proves
\sum_{s=1}^{n}v_{j,s}=\lambda=\sum_{s=1}^{n}v_{s,i}
. Of course, then
\sum_{s=1}^{n}v_{j,s}-\sum_{s=1}^{n}v_{s,i}=\lambda-\lambda=0
for any
\{i,j\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
.

Conversely, if
\sum_{s=1}^{n}v_{j,s}-\sum_{s=1}^{n}v_{s,i}=0
for any
\{i,j\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
and if we let
\lambda:=\sum_{s=1}^{n}v_{1,s}
and
w:=v-\lambda I
, then for any
\{i,j\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
, first,
\lambda=\sum_{s=1}^{n}v_{j,s}=\sum_{s=1}^{n}v_{s,i}
and thus, second,
\sum_{s=1}^{n}w_{j,s}=\sum_{s=1}^{n}(v_{j,s}-\lambda\delta_{j,s})=\sum_{s=1}^{n}v_{j,s}-\lambda=0
and, likewise,
\sum_{s=1}^{n}w_{s,i}=\sum_{s=1}^{n}(v_{s,i}-\lambda\delta_{s,i})=\sum_{s=1}^{n}v_{s,i}-\lambda=0
. Hence,
w
is small then.

(b)
If for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
the matrix
w:=v-\lambda I
is skew-symmetric, then
0=w_{j,i}+w_{i,j}=(v_{j,i}-\lambda\delta_{j,i})+(v_{i,j}-\lambda\delta_{i,j})=v_{j,i}+v_{i,j}-2\lambda\delta_{j,i}
for any
\{i,j\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
. Consequently, if
i\neq j
, this means
0=v_{j,i}+v_{i,j}
and, if
i=j
, we find
0=2v_{i,i}-2\lambda
, i.e.,
\lambda=v_{i,i}
. And that implies in particular
v_{j,j}-v_{i,i}=\lambda-\lambda=0
for any
\{i,j\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
.

If, conversely,
v_{j,i}+v_{i,j}=0
and
v_{j,j}-v_{i,i}=0
for any
\{i,j\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
with
i\neq j
and if we let
\lambda:=v_{1,1}
and
w:=v-\lambda I
, then, on the one hand,
\lambda=v_{i,i}
for any
i\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
and, on the other hand, for any
\{i,j\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
, generally,
w_{j,i}+w_{i,j}=(v_{j,i}-\lambda\delta_{j,i})+(v_{i,j}-\lambda\delta_{i,j})=v_{j,i}+v_{i,j}-2\lambda\delta_{j,i}
, which in case
i\neq j
simply means
w_{j,i}+w_{i,j}=v_{j,i}+v_{i,j}=0
and which for
i=j
amounts to
w_{j,i}+w_{i,j}=2v_{i,i}-2\lambda=2\lambda-2\lambda=0
. In conclusion,
w
is skew-symmetric then.

(c)
One implication is clear. If, conversely,
\{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\}\subseteq\mathbb{C}
are such that
v-\lambda_{1}I
is skew-symmetric and
v-\lambda_{2}I
is small, then
\lambda_{1}=v_{1,1}
by

(
b
)

and
\lambda_{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}v_{j,1}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{1,j}
by

(
a
)

. Subtracting the two identities
\sum_{j=1}^{n}v_{j,1}=\lambda_{1}+\sum_{j=2}^{n}v_{j,1}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{1,i}=\lambda_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{n}v_{1,i}
from each other therefore yields
0=\sum_{j=2}^{n}v_{j,1}-\sum_{i=2}^{n}v_{1,i}
. Since also
v_{i,1}=-v_{1,i}
for each
i\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
with
1<i
by

(
b
)

, that is the same as saying
0=2\sum_{j=2}^{n}v_{j,1}
. And
\sum_{j=2}^{n}v_{j,1}=0
then implies
\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{1}+\sum_{j=2}^{n}v_{j,1}=\lambda_{1}
, which is all we needed to see.
∎

Lemma 5.3
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and each statement
A
below, the set
\{v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\,\wedge\,A(v)\}
is a complex vector subspace of
M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
and has the listed dimension.

A(v)

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\{v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\,\wedge\,A(v)\}

(a)

\top

n^{2}

(b)

\exists\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\colon v-\lambda I
is small

(n-1)^{2}+1

(c)

v
is small

(n-1)^{2}

(d)

\exists\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\colon v-\lambda I
is skew-symmetric

{\frac{1}{2}}n(n-1)+1

(e)

v
is skew-symmetric

{\frac{1}{2}}n(n-1)

(f)

\exists\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\colon v-\lambda I
is skew-symmetric and small

{\frac{1}{2}}(n-1)(n-2)+1

(g)

v
is skew-symmetric and small

{\frac{1}{2}}(n-1)(n-2)

(h)

v
is diagonal

n

(i)

\exists\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\colon v-\lambda I=0

1

(j)

v=0

0

Proof.

(a)
It is well known that, if for any
\{k,\ell\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
the matrix
E^{n}_{\ell,k}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
has
\delta_{\ell,j}\delta_{k,i}
as its
(j,i)
-entry for any
\{i,j\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
, then the family
(E^{n}_{\ell,k})_{(\ell,k)\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket^{\times 2}}
is a
\mathbb{C}
-linear basis of
\{v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\,\wedge\,A(v)\}=M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
.

(b)
Since
A
can be expressed by a homogenous system of linear equations by Lemma
5.2

(
a
)

the set
\{v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\,\wedge\,A(v)\}
is indeed a vector space. Hence, it suffices to show that the mapping
\varphi_{n}\colon M_{n-1}(\mathbb{C})\oplus\mathbb{C}\to\{v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\,\wedge\,A(v)\}
defined by the rule that
(u,\lambda)\mapsto v
, where for any
\{i,j\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
,

v_{j,i}=\begin{cases}u_{j,i}+\lambda\delta_{j,i},&j<n\,\wedge\,i<n,\\
\displaystyle-\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}u_{\ell,i},&j=n\,\wedge\,i<n,\\[2.84526pt]
\displaystyle-\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}u_{j,k},&j<n\,\wedge\,i=n,\\[2.84526pt]
\displaystyle\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n-1}u_{\ell,k}+\lambda,&j=n\,\wedge\,i=n,\end{cases}

for any
u\in M_{n-1}(\mathbb{C})
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
, is a
\mathbb{C}
-linear isomorphism. We begin by proving that
\varphi_{n}
is well defined. For any
u\in M_{n-1}(\mathbb{C})
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
, if
\varphi_{n}(u,\lambda)=v
and if
w=v-\lambda I
, then, on the one hand, for any
i\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
with
i<n
, by definition,

\sum_{j=1}^{n}w_{j,i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(v_{j,i}-\lambda\delta_{j,i})+v_{n,i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}u_{j,i}+\left(-\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}u_{\ell,i}\right)=0.

and also,

\sum_{j=1}^{n}w_{j,n}=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(v_{j,n}-\lambda\delta_{j,n})+(v_{n,n}-\lambda)=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\left(-\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}u_{j,k}\right)+\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n-1}u_{\ell,k}=0.

On the other hand, for any
j\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
with
j<n
,

\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{j,i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(v_{j,i}-\lambda\delta_{j,i})+v_{j,n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}u_{j,i}+\left(-\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}u_{j,k}\right)=0

and also,

\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{n,i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(v_{n,i}-\lambda\delta_{n,i})+(v_{n,n}-\lambda)=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\left(-\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}u_{\ell,i}\right)+\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n-1}u_{\ell,k}=0.

Together these four conclusions prove that
w
is small, i.e., that
A(v)
holds.

Conversely, by Lemma
5.2

(
a
)

a well-defined
\mathbb{C}
-linear map
\psi_{n}\colon\{v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\,\wedge\,A(v)\}\to M_{n-1}(\mathbb{C})\oplus\mathbb{C}
is obtained as follows: For any
v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
with
A(v)
, if
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
is such that
v-\lambda I
is small, then
v\mapsto(u,\lambda)
, where for any
\{k,\ell\}\subseteq\llbracket{n-1}\rrbracket
,

u_{\ell,k}=v_{\ell,k}-\lambda\delta_{\ell,k}.

It remains to show
\psi_{n}\circ\varphi_{n}=\mathrm{id}
and
\varphi_{n}\circ\psi_{n}=\mathrm{id}
. And, indeed, for any
u\in M_{n-1}(\mathbb{C})
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
, if
v=\varphi_{n}(u,\lambda)
, then we have already seen that
w=v-\lambda I
is small. For any
\{k,\ell\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
, by definition,
w_{\ell,k}=v_{\ell,k}-\lambda\delta_{\ell,k}=(u_{\ell,k}+\lambda\delta_{\ell,k})-\lambda\delta_{\ell,k}=u_{\ell,k}
, which proves
\varphi_{n}(v)=(u,\lambda)
and thus
\psi_{n}\circ\varphi_{n}=\mathrm{id}
.

Conversely, for any
v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
such that
A(v)
is satisfied, if
(u,\lambda)=\psi_{n}(v)
, then we already know
\lambda=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}v_{\ell,i}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}v_{j,k}
for any
\{k,\ell\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
by Lemma
5.2

(
a
)

. If
v^{\prime}=\varphi_{n}(u,\lambda)
, then for any
\{i,j\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
with
i<n
and
j<n
it hence follows by definition
v^{\prime}_{j,i}=u_{j,i}+\lambda\delta_{j,i}=(v_{j,i}-\lambda\delta_{j,i})+\lambda\delta_{j,i}=v_{j,i}
as well as by
\lambda=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}v_{\ell,i}
,

v^{\prime}_{n,i}=-\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}u_{\ell,i}=-\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}(v_{\ell,i}-\lambda\delta_{\ell,i})=\lambda-\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}v_{\ell,i}=v_{n,i}

and by
\lambda=\sum_{k=1}^{n}v_{k,j}
,

v^{\prime}_{j,n}=-\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}u_{j,k}=-\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}(v_{j,k}-\lambda\delta_{j,k})=\lambda-\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}v_{j,k}=v_{j,n}

and, lastly,

\displaystyle v^{\prime}_{n,n}

\displaystyle{}=\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n-1}u_{\ell,k}+\lambda=\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n-1}(v_{\ell,k}-\lambda\delta_{\ell,k})+\lambda=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}v_{\ell,k}-\lambda\right)+\lambda

\displaystyle{}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}(-v_{\ell,n})+\lambda=v_{n,n},

where we have used
\lambda=\sum_{k=1}^{n}v_{\ell,k}
for any
\ell\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
in the next-to-last step and
\lambda=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}v_{\ell,n}
in the last.
Thus, we have shown
v^{\prime}=v
and thus
\varphi_{n}\circ\psi_{n}=\mathrm{id}
, which concludes the proof in this case.

(c)
By Lemma
5.2

(
a
)

,
the space
\{v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\,\wedge\,A(v)\}
is exactly the image of
M_{n-1}(\mathbb{C})\oplus\{0\}
under
\varphi_{n}
.

(d) Lemma
5.2

(
b
)

showed that
A
can be equivalently expressed as a system of homogenous linear equations, thus proving
\{v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\,\wedge\,A(v)\}
to be a vector space. Let
\Gamma_{n}=\{(j,i)\mid\{i,j\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket\,\wedge\,j<i\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\varnothing\}
as well as
B^{n}_{(j,i)}=T^{n}_{j,i}=E^{n}_{j,i}-E^{n}_{i,j}
for any
\{i,j\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
with
j<i
and
B^{n}_{\varnothing}=I
. Then, the claim will be verified once we show that
(B^{n}_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{n}}
is a
\mathbb{C}
-linear basis of
\{v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\,\wedge\,A(v)\}
.

The family
(B^{n}_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{n}}
is
\mathbb{C}
-linearly independent. Indeed, if
(a_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{n}}\in\mathbb{C}^{\oplus\Gamma_{n}}
is such that
\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{n}}a_{\gamma}\,B^{n}_{\gamma}=0
, then by
I=\sum_{i=1}^{n}E^{n}_{i,i}
,

0=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(j,i)\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket^{\times 2}\\
{}\,\wedge\,j<i\end{subarray}}a_{(j,i)}(E^{n}_{j,i}-E^{n}_{i,j})+a_{\varnothing}\sum_{i=1}^{n}E^{n}_{i,i}=\sum_{(j,i)\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket^{\times 2}}\begin{cases}\begin{cases}a_{(j,i)},&j<i\\
-a_{(i,j)},&i<j\\
a_{\varnothing},&j=i\end{cases}\end{cases}E^{n}_{j,i},

which demands
(a_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{n}}=0
since
(E^{n}_{\ell,k})_{(\ell,k)\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket^{\times 2}}
is
\mathbb{C}
-linearly independent.

It remains to prove that
\{B^{n}_{\gamma}\mid\gamma\in\Gamma_{n}\}
spans
\{v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\,\wedge\,A(v)\}
. If
v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
are such that
w=v-\lambda I
is skew-symmetric, then
v_{j,i}=-v_{i,j}
and
\lambda=v_{j,j}=v_{i,i}
for any
\{i,j\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
with
j\neq i
by Lemma
5.2

(
b
)

. Hence, if we let
a_{\varnothing}=\lambda
and
a_{(j,i)}=w_{j,i}=v_{j,i}
for any
\{i,j\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
with
j<i
, then

\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{n}}a_{\gamma}\,B^{n}_{\gamma}=\sum_{(j,i)\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket^{\times 2}}\begin{cases}\begin{cases}a_{(j,i)},&j<i\\
-a_{(i,j)},&i<j\\
a_{\varnothing},&j=i\end{cases}\end{cases}E^{n}_{j,i}=\sum_{(j,i)\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket^{\times 2}}\begin{cases}\begin{cases}v_{j,i},&j<i\\
-v_{i,j},&i<j\\
\lambda,&j=i\end{cases}\end{cases}E^{n}_{j,i}=v.

Thus,
(B^{n}_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{n}}
is a
\mathbb{C}
-linear basis.

(e)
The proof of the previous claim shows that any
v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
is skew-symmetric if and only if it is in the span of
\{B^{n}_{\gamma}\mid\gamma\in\Gamma_{n}\}
and has coefficient
0
with respect to
B^{n}_{\varnothing}
. Hence,
\{T^{n}_{j,i}\mid\{i,j\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket\,\wedge\,j<i\}
is a
\mathbb{C}
-linear basis of
\{v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\,\wedge\,A(v)\}
.

(f)
All three parts

(
a
)

–

(
c
)

of Lemma
5.2
combined imply that
\{v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\,\wedge\,A(v)\}
is the solution set to a homogenous system of linear equations and thus a vector space. Hence, it suffices to prove that
\varphi_{n}
restricts to a mapping
\{u\in M_{n-1}(\mathbb{C})\,\wedge\,u=-u^{\mathrm{t}}\}\to\{v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\,\wedge\,A(v)\}
and
\psi_{n}
to one in the reverse direction.

For any skew-symmetric
u\in M_{n-1}(\mathbb{C})
and any
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
, if
v=\varphi_{n}(u,\lambda)
and
w=v-\lambda I
, then for any
\{i,j\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
with
i<n
and
j<n
we have already seen that
w_{j,i}=u_{j,i}
, implying
w_{j,i}+w_{i,j}=u_{j,i}+u_{i,j}=0
by
u=-u^{\mathrm{t}}
. Moreover, for the same reason,

\displaystyle w_{n,i}+w_{i,n}

\displaystyle{}=(v_{n,i}-\lambda\delta_{n,i})+(v_{i,n}-\lambda\delta_{i,n})=v_{n,i}+v_{i,n}

\displaystyle{}=\left(-\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}u_{\ell,i}\right)+\left(-\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}u_{i,k}\right)=-\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}(u_{\ell,i}+u_{i,\ell})=0

and

\displaystyle w_{j,n}+w_{n,j}

\displaystyle{}=(v_{j,n}-\lambda\delta_{j,n})+(v_{n,j}-\lambda\delta_{n,j})=v_{j,n}+v_{n,j}

\displaystyle{}=\left(-\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}u_{j,k}\right)+\left(-\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}u_{\ell,j}\right)=-\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}(u_{j,k}+u_{k,j})=0

as well as

w_{n,n}+w_{n,n}=2(v_{n,n}-\lambda)=2\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n-1}u_{\ell,k}=\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n-1}(u_{\ell,k}+u_{k,\ell})=0,

which completes the proof that
\varphi_{n}
restricts to a map into
\{v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\,\wedge\,A(v)\}
.

Conversely, if
v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
are such that
w=v-\lambda I
is skew-symmetric and small, then
\lambda=v_{i,i}
for any
i\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
by Lemma
5.2

(
b
)

. For
(u,\lambda)=\psi_{n}(v)
and any
\{k,\ell\}\subseteq\llbracket{n-1}\rrbracket
, by definition,
u_{\ell,k}=w_{\ell,k}
and thus
u_{\ell,k}+u_{k,\ell}=w_{\ell,k}+w_{k,\ell}=0
by
w=-w^{\mathrm{t}}
. Hence,
\psi_{n}
maps
\{v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\,\wedge\,A(v)\}
into
\{u\in M_{n-1}(\mathbb{C})\,\wedge\,u=-u^{\mathrm{t}}\}\oplus\mathbb{C}
.

(g)
As we have just shown, any
v\in M_{\mathbb{C}}(n)
is skew-symmetric and small if and only if it lies in the image of
\{u\in M_{n-1}(\mathbb{C})\,\wedge\,u=-u^{\mathrm{t}}\}\oplus\{0\}
under
\varphi_{n}
. And
\varphi_{n}
is a
\mathbb{C}
-linear isomorphism from this space to
\{v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\,\wedge\,A(v)\}
.

(h)
It is well known that
(E^{n}_{i,i})_{i\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket}
is a
\mathbb{C}
-linear basis of
\{v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\,\wedge\,A(v)\}
.

(i)
In this case,
\{v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\,\wedge\,A(v)\}
is the
\mathbb{C}
-linear span of
I
in
M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
.

(j)
Here,
\{v\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\,\wedge\,A(v)\}
is the zero
\mathbb{C}
-linear space.
∎

6
First cohomology of unitary easy quantum group duals

This section computes the first quantum group cohomology with trivial coefficients (see Section
2
) of the discrete dual of any unitary easy compact quantum group (see Section
3
). That is achieved by applying the characterization of the first Hochschild cohomology recalled in Section
4
while using the results of Section
5
as auxiliaries.

6.1
Equations derived from the presentation

Resume the Assumptions
3.13
and the abbreviations from Notations
3.14
and
3.17
. In particular,
n
and
E
are then defined.
Remark
4.21
motivates moreover the following shorthand.

Notation 6.1
.

(a)

Let

Y
be the
\mathbb{C}\langle E\rangle
-bimodule
\mathbb{C}
with left and right actions given by
u^{\mathfrak{c}}_{j,i}\triangleright x:=\delta_{j,i}x
respectively
x\triangleleft u^{\mathfrak{c}}_{j,i}:=\delta_{j,i}x
for any
\{i,j\}\subseteq\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
, any
\mathfrak{c}\in\{\mathnormal{\circ},\mathnormal{\bullet}\}
and any
x\in\mathbb{C}
.

(b)

Let
F_{p}:=F^{1,p}_{E,\varnothing,Y}
for any
p\in\mathbb{C}\langle E\rangle
.

Then, by Section
4
for any category
\mathcal{C}
of two-colored partitions the first cohomology with trivial coefficients of the discrete dual of any easy quantum group associated with
(\mathcal{C},n)
can be realized as a solution space to a system of linear equations involving maps of the form
F_{r}
for certain
r\in\mathbb{C}\langle E\rangle
induced by
\mathcal{C}
and
n
.

Proposition 6.2
.

For any category
\mathcal{C}
of two-colored partitions,
if
G
is the unitary easy compact quantum group of
(\mathcal{C},n)
,
then there exists an isomorphism of
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}\bigl(\widehat{G}\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{x\in\mathbb{C}^{\times E}\,\wedge\,\forall r\in R_{\mathcal{C}}\colon F_{r}(x)=0\},}

which maps
(
the one-elemental cohomology class of
)
any
1
-cycle
\eta
to the tuple
x
with
x_{e}=\eta(e+J_{\mathcal{C}})
for any
e\in E
.

Proof.

By Definition
3.18
, the algebra underlying the Hopf
\ast
-algebra
\mathbb{C}[\widehat{G}]
is the universal algebra
\mathbb{C}\langle E\mid R_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle
. According to Section
2.2
, the vector space
H^{1}\bigl(\widehat{G}\bigr)
is defined as
H^{1}_{\mathrm{HS}}(\mathbb{C}\langle E\mid R_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle,X)
where
X={}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{C}_{\epsilon}
is trivial bimodule of
\mathbb{C}\langle E\mid R_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle
with respect to the counit
\epsilon
of
\mathbb{C}[\widehat{G}]
. By Proposition
3.20
, this counit is such that its restriction of scalars along the canonical projection
\mathbb{C}\langle E\rangle\to\mathbb{C}\langle E\mid R_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle
is precisely
Y
. Hence, the claim follows by
Example
4.20
and Remark
4.21
.
∎

The task laid out by Proposition
6.2
is clear. We need to solve the set of linear equations in
\mathbb{C}^{\times E}
on the right-hand side of the isomorphism there – for each category of two-colored partitions. Eventually, in Section
6.6
namely, solving these equations will require case distinctions for different kinds of categories of two-colored partitions. However, there are a great number of simplifications we can make to the equation system before it needs to come to that. Moreover, this reduces the number of cases we eventually have to consider immensely.

6.2
Simplifying each individual equation

As a first step towards solving the equations of Proposition
6.2
we consider each equation in isolation and simplify its definition. In other words, we seek a better formula for the values of the functional
F_{r}
for
r
of the form
\mathrm{r}^{\mathfrak{c}}_{{\mathfrak{d}}}(p)_{j,g}
for arbitrary two-colored partitions
(\mathfrak{c},{\mathfrak{d}},p)
and
g\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket^{\times|\mathfrak{c}|}
and
j\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket^{\times|{\mathfrak{d}}|}
.

It will be convenient to have a shorthand for mappings constructed by prescribing a specified value to a specified point and otherwise inheriting the graph of a given mapping with the same domain.

Notation 6.3
.

For any
\{k,\ell\}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, any mapping
f\colon\Pi^{k}_{\ell}\to\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
, any
\mathtt{z}\in\Pi^{k}_{\ell}
and any
s\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
write
f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s
for the mapping
\Pi^{k}_{\ell}\to\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
with
\mathtt{z}\mapsto s
and with
\mathtt{y}\mapsto f(\mathtt{y})
for any
\mathtt{y}\in\Pi^{k}_{\ell}\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}
.

Then, combining Notation
3.14
and Definition
4.11
yields the following description of the functionals we are investigating.

Lemma 6.4
.

For any
\{k,\ell\}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, any
\mathfrak{c}\in\{\mathnormal{\circ},\mathnormal{\bullet}\}^{\times k}
, any
\mathfrak{d}\in\{\mathnormal{\circ},\mathnormal{\bullet}\}^{\times\ell}
, any set-theoretical partition
p
of
\Pi^{k}_{\ell}
, any
g\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket^{\times k}
, any
j\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket^{\times\ell}
and any
x\in\mathbb{C}^{\times E}
, if
r=\mathrm{r}^{\mathfrak{c}}_{\mathfrak{d}}(p)_{j,g}
and
f=g\begin{smallmatrix}\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}\hskip 4.0pt\\
\hskip 4.0pt\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}\end{smallmatrix}j
and
\mathfrak{w}=\mathfrak{c}\begin{smallmatrix}\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}\hskip 4.0pt\\
\hskip 4.0pt\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}\end{smallmatrix}\mathfrak{d}
, then

F_{r}(x)=\sum_{\mathtt{z}\in\Pi^{k}_{\ell}}\sum_{s=1}^{n}\zeta(p,\ker(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s))\begin{cases}\begin{cases}-x_{u_{s,f(\mathtt{z})}^{\mathfrak{w}(\mathtt{z})}}&\text{if }\mathtt{z}\in\Pi^{k}_{0}\\
x_{u_{f(\mathtt{z}),s}^{\mathfrak{w}(\mathtt{z})}}&\text{if }\mathtt{z}\in\Pi^{0}_{\ell}\end{cases}\end{cases}.

Proof.

For any
x\in\mathbb{C}^{\times E}
, by Example
4.12
,

\displaystyle F_{r}(x)

\displaystyle{}=\sum_{i\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket^{\times\ell}}\zeta(p,\ker(g\begin{smallmatrix}\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}\hskip 4.0pt\\
\hskip 4.0pt\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}\end{smallmatrix}i))\sum_{b=1}^{\ell}\biggl(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\in\llbracket{\ell}\rrbracket\\
{}\,\wedge\,q\neq b\end{subarray}}\delta_{j_{b},i_{b}}\biggr)\;x_{u_{j_{b},i_{b}}^{\mathfrak{d}_{b}}}

\displaystyle\quad{}-\sum_{h\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket^{\times k}}\zeta(p,\ker(h\begin{smallmatrix}\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}\hskip 4.0pt\\
\hskip 4.0pt\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}\end{smallmatrix}j))\sum_{a=1}^{k}\biggl(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\in\llbracket{k}\rrbracket\\
{}\,\wedge\,q\neq a\end{subarray}}\delta_{h_{a},g_{a}}\biggr)\;x_{u_{h_{a},g_{a}}^{\mathfrak{c}_{a}}}.

After commuting the sums and evaluating the sums over
i
respectively
h
(as far as possible), this is identical to

\displaystyle\sum_{b=1}^{\ell}\sum_{i_{b}=1}^{n}\zeta(p,\ker(g\begin{smallmatrix}\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}\hskip 4.0pt\\
\hskip 4.0pt\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}\end{smallmatrix}(j_{1},\ldots,j_{b-1},i_{b},j_{b+1},\ldots,j_{\ell})))\;x_{u_{j_{b},i_{b}}^{\mathfrak{d}_{b}}}

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{a=1}^{k}\sum_{h_{a}=1}^{n}\zeta(p,\ker((g_{1},\ldots,g_{a-1},h_{a},g_{a+1},\ldots,g_{k})\begin{smallmatrix}\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}\hskip 4.0pt\\
\hskip 4.0pt\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}\end{smallmatrix}j))\;x_{u_{h_{a},g_{a}}^{\mathfrak{c}_{a}}}.

That agrees with the right-hand side of the claimed identity.
∎

While Lemma
6.4
has given a more concise form to the equations under investigation, it can be improved upon significantly.
Firstly, one can give a simpler criterion for when in the sum on the right-hand side of the identity in Lemma
6.4
a factor
\zeta(p,\ker(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s))
is non-zero.

Lemma 6.5
.

For any
\{k,\ell\}\!\subseteq\!{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, any set-theoretical partition
p
of
\Pi^{k}_{\ell}
, any mapping
{f\colon\!\Pi^{k}_{\ell}\!\to\!\llbracket{n}\rrbracket}
, any
\mathtt{z}\in\Pi^{k}_{\ell}
and any
s\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
,
the statements
p\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)
and

p\backslash\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\},\{\mathtt{z}\}\}\backslash\{\varnothing\}\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f)\quad\,\wedge\,\quad\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})

are equivalent.

Proof.

We show each implication separately. Below, we will use many times the fact that for any
{t\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket}
,

\displaystyle(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)^{\leftarrow}(\{t\})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}

\displaystyle{}=\{\mathtt{a}\in\Pi^{k}_{\ell}\,\wedge\,(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)(\mathtt{a})=t\,\wedge\,\mathtt{a}\neq\mathtt{z}\}

\displaystyle{}=\{\mathtt{a}\in\Pi^{k}_{\ell}\,\wedge\,f(\mathtt{a})=t\,\wedge\,\mathtt{a}\neq\mathtt{z}\}

\displaystyle{}=f^{\leftarrow}(\{t\})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}.

Step 1.

First, suppose
p\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)
. Then, there exists
t\in\mathrm{ran}(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)
such that
\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\subseteq(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)^{\leftarrow}(\{t\})
. Because
\mathtt{z}\in\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})
this requires
\mathtt{z}\in(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)^{\leftarrow}(\{t\})
and thus
t=s
by
{(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)(\mathtt{z})=s}
. It follows
\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\subseteq(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})
and thus in particular
\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}=f^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})
, which is one half of what we had to show.

It is trivially true that
\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{f(\mathtt{z})\})\in\ker(f)
. We have already seen that
\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})\in\ker(f)
. For any
\mathsf{B}\in p
with
\mathsf{B}\neq\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})
, i.e.,
\mathtt{z}\notin\mathsf{B}
, there exists by assumption
t^{\prime}\in\mathrm{ran}(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)
with
\mathsf{B}\subseteq(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)^{\leftarrow}(\{t^{\prime}\})
. We conclude
\mathsf{B}=\mathsf{B}\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)^{\leftarrow}(\{t^{\prime}\})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}=f^{\leftarrow}(\{t^{\prime}\})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{t^{\prime}\})\in\ker(f)
. Thus, the other half of the claim,
p\backslash\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\},\allowbreak\{\mathtt{z}\}\}\backslash\{\varnothing\}\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f)
, holds as well. That proves one implication.

Step 2.
In order to show the converse implication we assume that both
p\backslash\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\},\allowbreak\{\mathtt{z}\}\}\backslash\{\varnothing\}\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f)
and
\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})
and then we distinguish two cases.

Case 2.1.
If
\{\mathtt{z}\}\in p
and thus
\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})=\{\mathtt{z}\}
and
\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}=\varnothing
, then the assumption is simply equivalent to the statement
p\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f)
. Naturally,
\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})\in\ker(f)
by
(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)(\mathtt{z})=s
. For any
\mathsf{B}\in p
with
\mathsf{B}\neq\{\mathtt{z}\}
there exists by our premise a value
t\in\mathrm{ran}(f)
with
\mathsf{B}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{t\})
. Thus, also
\mathsf{B}=\mathsf{B}\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{t\})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}=(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)^{\leftarrow}(\{t\})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\in\ker(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)
. In conclusion,
p\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)
.

Case 2.2.
In the instance that
\{\mathtt{z}\}\notin p
the initial assumption simplifies to the statement
p\backslash\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\},\{\mathtt{z}\}\}\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f)
and
\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})
. The latter condition implies
\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}=(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})
and thus by
(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)(\mathtt{z})=s
also
\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})=\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})\cup\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})\in\ker(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)
. On the other hand, for any
\mathsf{B}\in p
with
\mathsf{B}\neq\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})
, which is to say
\mathtt{z}\notin\mathsf{B}
, there exists by assumption
t\in\mathrm{ran}(f)
with
\mathsf{B}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{t\})
. It follows
\mathsf{B}=\mathsf{B}\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{t\})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}=(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)^{\leftarrow}(\{t\})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)^{\leftarrow}(\{t\})\in\ker(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)
. Hence, altogether,
p\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma
6.5
can now be used to give a necessary criterion for the right-hand side of the identity in Lemma
6.4
to be non-zero as a whole. Namely,
p
and
f
must meet one of three conditions:

(i)

The labeling
f
maps any points belonging to the same element of
p
to the same value.

(ii)

There is an element of size two of
p
whose elements
f
maps to different values. Besides that
f
is as in (i).

(iii)

There is an element of
p
of size three or larger, all but one of whose elements are assigned the same value by
f
and whose remaining element
f
sends to a different value. Apart from that,
f
is as in (i).

Definition 6.6
.

Let
\{k,\ell\}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, let
p
be any set-theoretical partition of
\Pi^{k}_{\ell}
and let
f\colon\Pi^{k}_{\ell}\to\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
. Then, we say that
(p,f)
is

(a)

case R1
if
p\neq\varnothing
and
p\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f)
,

(b)

case R2
if there exists
\{\mathtt{z}_{1},\mathtt{z}_{2}\}\in p
such that
f(\mathtt{z}_{1})\neq f(\mathtt{z}_{2})
and such that for any
\mathsf{A}\in p
with
\mathsf{A}\neq\{\mathtt{z}_{1},\mathtt{z}_{2}\}
there is
\mathsf{B}\in\ker(f)
with
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\mathsf{B}
, in which case the set
\{\mathtt{z}_{1},\mathtt{z}_{2}\}
is called
critical data
of
(p,f)
,

(c)

case R3
if there exist
\mathsf{Z}\in p
and
\mathtt{z}\in\mathsf{Z}
and
s\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
such that
3\leq|\mathsf{Z}|
, such that
f(\mathtt{z})\neq s
, such that
f(\mathtt{y})=s
for any
\mathtt{y}\in\mathsf{Z}
with
\mathtt{y}\neq\mathtt{z}
and such that for any
\mathsf{A}\in p
with
\mathsf{A}\neq\mathsf{Z}
there is
\mathsf{B}\in\ker(f)
with
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\mathsf{B}
, in which case
(\mathsf{Z},\mathtt{z},s)
are called
critical data
of
(p,f)
,

(d)

case R4
otherwise.

Example 6.7
.

For
3\leq n
, consider
k:=4
and
\ell:=5
, the set-theoretical partition
p:=\{\{\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{1},\prescript{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{}{1}\},\allowbreak\{\prescript{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{}{2}\},\{\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{2},\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{3},\prescript{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{}{3}\},\{\prescript{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{}{4}\},\{\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{4},\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{5}\}\}
of
\Pi^{k}_{\ell}
and various different mappings
f\colon\Pi^{k}_{\ell}\to\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
which all have in common that
each of
\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{2}
,
\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{4}
and
\prescript{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{}{3}
is mapped to
1
, that each of
\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{1}
,
\prescript{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{}{1}
and
\prescript{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{}{2}
is mapped to
2
and that
\prescript{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{}{4}
is mapped to
3
. Thus, at most the values of
\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{3}
and
\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{5}
differ between different
f
:

\scriptstyle 2
\scriptstyle 2
\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle 3
\scriptstyle 2
\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle 1
f
p
f

(a)

If
f(\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{3})=f(\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{5})=1
, then
(p,f)
is case R1.

(b)

If
f(\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{3})=1
and
f(\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{5})=2
, if
\mathtt{z}_{1}:=\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{4}
and
\mathtt{z}_{2}:=\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{5}
, then
(p,f)
is case R2 with critical data
\{\mathtt{z}_{1},\mathtt{z}_{2}\}=\{\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{4},\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{5}\}
.

(c)

If
f(\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{3})=2
and
f(\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{5})=1
, if
\mathsf{Z}:=\{\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{2},\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{3},\prescript{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{}{3}\}
, if
\mathtt{z}:=\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{3}
and
s:=2
, then
(p,f)
is case R3 with critical data
(\mathsf{Z},\mathtt{z},s)=(\{\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{2},\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{3},\prescript{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{}{3}\},\{\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{3}\},2)
.

(d)

If
f(\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{3})=f(\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{5})=2
, then
(p,f)
is case R4.

Lemma 6.8
.

(a)

In each of the cases R2 and R3 critical data are unique.

(b)

The cases R1–R4 are mutually exclusive and exhaustive.

Proof.

Let
\{k,\ell\}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, let
p
be any set-theoretical partition of
\Pi^{k}_{\ell}
and let
f\colon\Pi^{k}_{\ell}\to\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
be arbitrary.

(a)

Case R2.
Suppose that
(p,f)
is case R2 and that both
\{\mathtt{z}_{1},\mathtt{z}_{2}\}
and
\{\mathtt{z}_{1}^{\prime},\mathtt{z}_{2}^{\prime}\}
are critical data of
(p,f)
. If
\{\mathtt{z}_{1},\mathtt{z}_{2}\}\neq\{\mathtt{z}_{1}^{\prime},\mathtt{z}_{2}^{\prime}\}
were true, then by the assumption on
\{\mathtt{z}_{1},\mathtt{z}_{2}\}
there would exist
\mathsf{B}\in\ker(f)
with
\{\mathtt{z}_{1}^{\prime},\mathtt{z}_{2}^{\prime}\}\subseteq\mathsf{B}
, meaning
f(\mathtt{z}_{1}^{\prime})=f(\mathtt{z}_{2}^{\prime})
, contrarily to our assumption. Hence,
\{\mathtt{z}_{1},\mathtt{z}_{2}\}=\{\mathtt{z}_{1}^{\prime},\mathtt{z}_{2}^{\prime}\}
must be true instead.

Case R3.
Now, let
(p,f)
be case R3 and let both
(\mathsf{Z},\mathtt{z},s)
and
(\mathsf{Z}^{\prime},\mathtt{z}^{\prime},s^{\prime})
be critical data of
(p,f)
.
If
\mathsf{Z}\neq\mathsf{Z}^{\prime}
held, the assumption on
\mathsf{Z}
would imply the existence of
\mathsf{B}\in\ker(f)
with
\mathsf{Z}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{B}
. In particular, it would follow
f(\mathtt{y}^{\prime})=f(\mathtt{z}^{\prime})
for any
\mathtt{y}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Z}^{\prime}
with
\mathtt{y}^{\prime}\neq\mathtt{z}^{\prime}
, of which there exists at least one by
3\leq|\mathsf{Z}^{\prime}|
. Because that would contradict the assumption, we must have
\mathsf{Z}=\mathsf{Z}^{\prime}
instead.

Furthermore, supposing
\mathtt{z}\neq\mathtt{z}^{\prime}
demands of any
\mathtt{y}\in\mathsf{Z}\backslash\{\mathtt{z},\mathtt{z}^{\prime}\}
both
f(\mathtt{y})=s
by the assumption on
\mathtt{z}
and
s
and
f(\mathtt{y})=s^{\prime}
by the one on
\mathtt{z}^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime}
. Hence, as
\mathsf{Z}\backslash\{\mathtt{z},\mathtt{z}^{\prime}\}\neq\varnothing
by
3\leq|\mathsf{Z}^{\prime}|
, if
\mathtt{z}^{\prime}\neq\mathtt{z}
, then
s=s^{\prime}
. That would be a contradiction because the property of
\mathtt{z}^{\prime}
also requires
s\neq f(\mathtt{z})=s^{\prime}
in that case. Hence, only
\mathtt{z}=\mathtt{z}^{\prime}
can be true.

Lastly, because the assumptions on
s
and
s^{\prime}
imply
f(\mathtt{y})=s
respectively
f(\mathtt{y})=s^{\prime}
for any
\mathtt{y}\in\mathsf{Z}
with
\mathtt{y}\neq\mathtt{z}=\mathtt{z}^{\prime}
and because
\mathsf{Z}\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\neq\varnothing
, we must have
s=s^{\prime}
as well.

(b)
It is enough to prove that cases R1–R3 are mutually exclusive.
If
(p,f)
is case R2, then it cannot be case R1 because
f(\mathtt{z}_{1})\neq f(\mathtt{z}_{2})
excludes the existence of
\mathsf{B}\in\ker(f)
with
\{\mathtt{z}_{1},\mathtt{z}_{2}\}\subseteq\mathsf{B}
, which would be necessary for
p\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f)
to hold.
Similarly,
(p,f)
being case R3 forbids it being case R1 as well because the existence of
\mathtt{y}\in\mathsf{Z}\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\neq\varnothing
with
f(\mathtt{z})\neq s=f(\mathtt{y})
does not allow any
\mathsf{B}\in\ker(f)
with
\mathsf{Z}\subseteq\mathsf{B}
to exist, which
p\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f)
would require.
Lastly, if
(p,f)
were simultaneously case R2 and case R3, then
\{\mathtt{z}_{1},\mathtt{z}_{2}\}\neq\mathsf{Z}
would follow from
3\leq|\mathsf{Z}|
, thus demanding by the property of
\mathsf{Z}
the existence of
\mathsf{B}\in\ker(f)
with
\{\mathtt{z}_{1},\mathtt{z}_{2}\}\subseteq\mathsf{B}
, in contradiction to
f(\mathtt{z}_{1})\neq f(\mathtt{z}_{2})
.
∎

Lemma 6.9
.

For any
{\{k,\ell\}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}
, any set-theoretical partition
p
of
\Pi^{k}_{\ell}
and any mapping
f\colon\Pi^{k}_{\ell}\to\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
there exist
\mathtt{z}\in\Pi^{k}_{\ell}
and
s\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
such that
p\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)
if and only if
(p,f)
is not case R4.

Proof.

Each implication is shown individually.

Step 1.
First, we suppose that
(p,f)
is not case R4
and deduce the existence of
\mathtt{z}\in\Pi^{k}_{\ell}
and
s\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
with
p\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)
. By Lemma
6.5
, that is the same as finding
\mathtt{z}\in\Pi^{k}_{\ell}
and
s\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
such that
p\backslash\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\},\{\mathtt{z}\}\}\backslash\{\varnothing\}\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f)
and
\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})
. By Lemma
6.8

(
b
)

, the pair
(p,f)
is case R1, case R2 or case R3. These three cases are treated individually.

Case 1.1.

If
(p,f)
is case R1, then by
p\neq\varnothing
we can find and fix some
\mathtt{z}\in\Pi^{k}_{\ell}
and put
s:=f(\mathtt{z})
. From
p\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f)
, it then follows
\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})
and thus in particular
\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})
, which is one part of what we have to show. The other part,
p\backslash\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\},\{\mathtt{z}\}\}\backslash\{\varnothing\}\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f)
is a consequence of the fact
p\backslash\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\},\{\mathtt{z}\}\}\backslash\{\varnothing\}\mathrel{\leq}p
and the assumption
p\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f)
.

Case 1.2.
Next, let
(p,f)
be case R2 and let
\{\mathtt{z}_{1},\mathtt{z}_{2}\}
be its critical data.
If we define
\mathtt{z}:=\mathtt{z}_{1}
and
s:=f(\mathtt{z}_{2})
, then
\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})=\{\mathtt{z}_{1},\mathtt{z}_{2}\}
and thus
\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}=\{\mathtt{z}_{2}\}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})
. On the other hand,
p\backslash\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\},\{\mathtt{z}\}\}\backslash\{\varnothing\}=p\backslash\{\{\mathtt{z}_{1},\mathtt{z}_{2}\}\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\{\mathtt{z}_{1}\},\{\mathtt{z}_{2}\}\}\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f)
because, by assumption, for each
\mathsf{A}\in p\backslash\{\{\mathtt{z}_{1},\mathtt{z}_{2}\}\}
there exists
\mathsf{B}\in\ker(f)
with
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\mathsf{B}\in\ker(f)
and, of course,
\{\mathtt{z}_{1}\}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{f(\mathtt{z}_{1})\})\in\ker(f)
and
\{\mathtt{z}_{2}\}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})
.

Case 1.3.
Finally, let
(p,f)
be case R3 and let
(\mathsf{Z},\mathtt{z},s)
be its critical data. Then, obviously,
\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}=\mathsf{Z}\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})
by assumption. And,
p\backslash\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\},\{\mathtt{z}\}\}\backslash\{\varnothing\}=p\backslash\{\mathsf{Z}\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\mathsf{Z}\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\},\{\mathtt{z}\}\}\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f)
because, by assumption, for any
\mathsf{A}\in p\backslash\{\mathsf{Z}\}
there exists
\mathsf{B}\in\ker(f)
with
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\mathsf{B}
and because
\mathsf{Z}\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})\in\ker(f)
and
\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{f(\mathtt{z})\})\in\ker(f)
. That proves one implication.

Step 2.
In order to show the converse implication, we assume that there exist
\mathtt{z}\in\Pi^{k}_{\ell}
and
s\in\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
such that
p\backslash\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\},\{\mathtt{z}\}\}\backslash\{\varnothing\}\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f)
and
\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})
(which we can by Lemma
6.5
) and derive that
(p,f)
is case R1, case R2 or case R3, thus proving that
(p,f)
is not case R4 by Lemma
6.8

(
b
)

.
Note that the existence of
\mathtt{z}
requires
p\neq\varnothing
. Again, a case distinction is in order.

Case 2.1.
First, let
f(\mathtt{z})=s
. Then
\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})=\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})\in\ker(f)
by
\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})
. Thus,
p\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f)
by
p\backslash\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\},\{\mathtt{z}\}\}\backslash\{\varnothing\}\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f)
. In other words, we have shown
(p,f)
to be case R1.

Case 2.2.
Similarly, if
\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})=\{\mathtt{z}\}
, then
p=p\backslash\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\},\{\mathtt{z}\}\}\backslash\{\varnothing\}\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f)
. Thus,
(p,f)
is case R1.

Case 2.3.
If
f(\mathtt{z})\neq s
and
|\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})|=2
, then we put
\mathtt{z}_{1}:=\mathtt{z}
and we let
\mathtt{z}_{2}
be the unique element of
\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}
. It follows
\{\mathtt{z}_{2}\}=\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})
and thus
f(\mathtt{z}_{2})=s\neq f(\mathtt{z})=f(\mathtt{z}_{1})
by our assumptions. And, the premise
p\backslash\{\{\mathtt{z}_{1},\mathtt{z}_{2}\}\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\{\mathtt{z}_{1}\},\{\mathtt{z}_{2}\}\}=p\backslash\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\},\{\mathtt{z}\}\}\backslash\{\varnothing\}\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f)
means that for any
\mathsf{A}\in p
with
\mathsf{A}\neq\{\mathtt{z}_{1},\mathtt{z}_{2}\}
there exists
\mathsf{B}\in\ker(f)
with
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\mathsf{B}
. Hence,
(p,f)
is case R2 with critical data
\{\mathtt{z}_{1},\mathtt{z}_{2}\}
.

Case 2.4.
The last remaining possibility is that
f(\mathtt{z})\neq s
and
3\leq|\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})|
. Putting
\mathsf{Z}:=\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})
implies
\mathsf{Z}\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}=\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\}\subseteq f^{\leftarrow}(\{s\})
by assumption, which is to say
f(\mathtt{y})=s\neq f(\mathtt{z})
for any
\mathtt{y}\in\mathsf{Z}
with
\mathtt{y}\neq\mathtt{z}
. On the other hand, since
p\backslash\{\mathsf{Z}\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\mathsf{Z}\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\},\{\mathtt{z}\}\}=p\backslash\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\pi_{p}(\mathtt{z})\backslash\{\mathtt{z}\},\{\mathtt{z}\}\}\backslash\{\varnothing\}\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f)
, for any
\mathsf{A}\in p
with
\mathsf{A}\neq\mathsf{Z}
there exists
\mathsf{B}\in\ker(f)
with
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\mathsf{B}
. In other words,
(p,f)
is case R3 with critical data
(\mathsf{Z},\mathtt{z},s)
. Thus, both implications are true.
∎

Example 6.10
.

For each of the first three
f
of Example
6.7
(but not the fourth) one can give at least one
(\mathtt{z},s)\in\Pi^{k}_{\ell}\times\llbracket{n}\rrbracket
such that
p\mathrel{\leq}\ker(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)
(and, in fact,
\ker(f\downarrow_{\mathtt{z}}s)=\{\{\prescript{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{}{1},\prescript{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{}{2},\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{1}\},\{\prescript{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{}{3},\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{2},\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{3},\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{4},\prescript{}{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{5}\},\{\prescript{\resizebox{180780}{}{$\blacksquare$}}{}{4}\}\}
).
